> 5. **Characterize the renderings**: the renderer, what stays invariant across it, the multiplicity and rooted complexity that make ours dominant, and observer persistence (§XIII.6).
> 6. **Tie gravity to rendering**: gravity as a quotient of each rendering, the compatibility $r_u\circ U_u = \bar U_u\circ q$, and a local split of Weyl curvature for the firewall (§XIII.9).
> 7. **Build the optical readout**: a connection on the correspondence, coherence through detector overlap, closure obstruction, and prospect holonomy with curvature odd under the plane reflection — the route to the optical cap problem (§XIII.2–XIII.3, §XIII.8–XIII.9, and §XI.2's item 0).
> 8. **Account for the galactic plane and the spirals**: the fixed-set involution, and the flip as readout monodromy, reconciled with coherent rotation (§XIII.7).
> 9. **Do the constructions available now**: glue the local models of $E$ into a global one, starting at the poles (§II.2); write an explicit prospect connection; construct gauge-invariant chiral weights.
> 10. **Decide the conjugate gate**: the higher-order completion at a Jacobi zero mode, the passage of a critical family through the correspondence, and one observable that differs from standard theory — with the twin-located antipodal family, which must not render as a self-image, as the first test (§XIII.10).
> 11. **Decide the modular-return branch** (Part XIV): a return map with discrete stable classes, a unitary flux redistribution, a non-exact dilation cocycle whose scale is derived rather than inserted, and an invariant that puts the self-return class in the readout kernel — or fail it cleanly on its ledger.
> 12. **Give the bi-connection its dynamics** (Part XV): a sign rule from the substrate, an action for $A$ with the exchange symmetry, the flux on return-map islands and the constancy of $\eta/D_{\rm app}$, and the frequency readout of a length homothety — then the fiber-product form of $\mathcal M$ and both field sectors from one null structure.
> 13. **Decide whether the three $\mathbb C^\times$'s are one** (§XV.6): the charge branch's $w$, Berry's phase with a size, and the two-leg holonomy — and whether the pair's exchange is the twin.
> 14. **Build the sheets** (Part XVI): the compatibility functional on the broken family, the fiber experiment, the Hessian normal derived before it is compared with the sky, and the Hubble derivative $d\eta/dD_{\rm app}$ on a family of sheets — after Part XV's calculations, because a sheet's $\eta$ is Part XV's.
> 15. **Test the equal-footing branch** (Part XVII): whether the Hopf data carry the disk's rotation sense, the twin parity as angular momentum, the global split quotient — and retire it if it merely doubles the arbitrary choices.
> 16. **Keep empirical discipline throughout**: derive any preferred axis before comparing it with the dipole; leave the sky toward the galactic poles bright; judge the detached wing only by its acceptance test (Part XII).
## XIII.1 Three things version 16 already contains [D/V]

Before any new structure is added, the frozen architecture already realizes three of these intuitions in miniature.

**One class, two appearances.** The representational slice double-covers the gravity slice: each gravity-base point $b$ appears twice, as $x$ and its twin $\tau x$, and the two appearances carry different content — the matter at $x$, none at $\tau x$. That is the agreeing-structure picture exactly: one gravitational class, two appearances retaining different parts of it, which light reads as two places. **The seed is the first instance** (§XIII.4).

**A family of renderings over one gravity base.** Swapping source and twin on a meridian leaves the reduced source $T^{(g)}$ — a sum over both appearances — unchanged, and so leaves $B_{\rm grav}$ unchanged. With the conditional selection rule of §V.4 there are $2^N$ occupancy assignments for $N$ meridians, all rendering over one and the same gravity base. This realizes, modestly, the strongest answer to the invariance question of §XIII.6: $B_{\rm grav}[U] = B_{\rm grav}$ for every rendering $U$.

**A budget that is not a product.** The Hopf classes at the crossings are $\pm1$ each, but Kirchhoff's condition forces their total to vanish, so flipping one forces a compensating flip elsewhere. The admissible sign patterns — 6 for two meridians, 70 for four — do not factor meridian by meridian (§V.4). That is a verified, if tiny, whack-a-mole budget: local states under a global, non-product compatibility condition.

## XIII.2 Canonical optical kinematics [T/D/O]

**The global object is a correspondence [D].** An ordinary map cannot render one underlying point twice, so globally the optical object is the incidence correspondence of §XIII.4, $\mathfrak X\xleftarrow{s}\mathcal I_U\xrightarrow{t}V_U$, and the optical connection lives on its right leg, $T\mathcal I_U = H_{\rm opt}\oplus\ker dt$. A local branch $\sigma$ of the correspondence gives a map $Q_{\rm opt} = t\circ\sigma$, and the machinery below applies branch by branch. **Every map written in this part from the substrate to a rendering — $Q_{\rm opt}$, $\Pi_s$, $U_u$ — is to be read in this branchwise sense.** Canonical optics and agreeing structure are then two faces of one object rather than neighbours.

**The state space and the readout, on a branch [D].** For canonical optics the substrate is taken to be $E$ itself, so locally $s:\mathcal I_U\to E$ admits a section $\sigma$ and $Q_{\rm opt} = t\circ\sigma:E\to V_U$. A canonical optical state is a point $e\in E$ with a direction $[v]$ whose image $[dQ_{\rm opt}(v)]$ is null in the optical metric; the states form a null-projective subbundle of $\mathbb{P}(TE)$. The readout at resolution $R$ is

$$\mu_R(e,[v]) = \mathcal C_R\big(Q_{\rm opt}(e),\,[dQ_{\rm opt}(v)]\big)$$

where $\mathcal C_R$ coarse-grains directions at the resolution of the physically available channel. **Rule A**, an Aethic structural stipulation rather than a requirement of quantum mechanics: $Q_{\rm opt}(e_1) = Q_{\rm opt}(e_2)$ exactly. Quantum mechanics itself asks only for a nonzero detector overlap, $\langle\Psi_2|E_y|\Psi_1\rangle\neq0$ (§XIII.3); the theory deliberately imposes more. **Rule B:** the angle between $[dQ_{\rm opt}(v_1)]$ and $[dQ_{\rm opt}(v_2)]$ is below $\epsilon_\theta(R)$.

> **A type subtlety [D].** Rule B defines a *tolerance* relation, not an equivalence: $v_1\approx v_2$ and $v_2\approx v_3$ need not give $v_1\approx v_3$. Turning "observational equivalence" into an equivalence relation requires partitioning directions into resolution cells, which then declares two nearly identical directions distinct whenever a cell boundary happens to separate them. Physically, distinguishability is graded rather than binary; the clean object is the overlap of records (§XIII.3), with rule B as its coarse shadow.

**Connection, curvature and holonomy [T].** With the vertical bundle $V_{\rm opt} = \ker dQ_{\rm opt}$ and a horizontal complement, $TE = H_{\rm opt}\oplus V_{\rm opt}$, every base path lifts uniquely to a horizontal path through each starting point. The curvature $\Omega_{\rm opt}$ — the vertical part of the bracket of horizontal lifts — measures the failure of those lifts to close: lifting a small loop of area $A$ returns to a point displaced vertically by about $\Omega_{\rm opt}\cdot A$, and for principal connections the Ambrose–Singer theorem makes the holonomy algebra the one generated by curvature transported along the lifts. This gives the heuristic $\|\Omega_{\rm opt}\|L^2\ll1$ its invariant form: **new effects are suppressed exactly when the holonomy of every loop an experiment can close lies below that experiment's resolution.** "Same measured endpoint, different canonical endpoint" — $Q_{\rm opt}({\rm Hol}_\gamma(e)) = Q_{\rm opt}(e)$ with ${\rm Hol}_\gamma(e)\neq e$ — is precisely nontrivial holonomy. What is needed is not curvature small here and large there — curvature is local — but **local loop holonomy negligible and arena-scale integrated holonomy of order one**, $H(\gamma)\sim\exp\big(\int_\Sigma\Omega_{\rm opt}\big)$, which weak curvature integrated over large surfaces can supply. Whether the connection does this is to be derived, not assumed **[O]**.

**Which base optics resolves [D/O].** The seed says light resolves a representative in $B_{\rm rep}$. But version 16 supplies no projection onto $B_{\rm rep}$: it is embedded in the middle space, not a quotient of it. The natural candidate, retraction along the meridians, is two-valued, because each meridian crosses $B_{\rm rep}$ at a source and at its twin. **So an optical readout through the meridians is itself a correspondence, not a map** (§XIII.4). A separate optical base is needed only if the optical geometry must depart from $r^*g_G$ globally — the problem of §XI.2's item 0.

**The two hidden directions [D].** If the vertical bundle contains both the Hopf-phase and the meridian directions, their holonomies act very differently: holonomy along the Hopf phase shifts a *phase* — the same state, reached with a different phase — while holonomy along the meridian displaces the canonical *position*, producing a different state. The next section shows why that difference decides the branch.

## XIII.3 Coherence: what geometry can and cannot change [T/D]

**Standard quantum mechanics [T].** The probability of an outcome $y$ is

$$P(y) = \sum_{\alpha,\beta}A_\alpha A_\beta^*\,\langle\Psi_\beta|E_y|\Psi_\alpha\rangle$$

with $E_y$ the detector's POVM element and $|\Psi_\alpha\rangle$ the complete final states, environment included. A cross term survives whenever the alternatives keep a nonzero overlap *as seen by the detector and the environment*. Exact identity of final states is sufficient but not necessary: distinct wavepackets can be nonorthogonal, a coarse measurement can fail to separate states that are not gauge-equivalent, and in quantum erasure a distinction disappears precisely at the level that matters. Interference is lost only when the alternatives are orthogonal as far as everything that registers them is concerned. The coherent class amplitude $\mathcal A(Y_i,Y_f) = \int_{\mu_R(\gamma(1)) = Y_f}\mathcal D\gamma\,e^{iS/\hbar}$ is therefore the right object exactly when the members of the class overlap under the detector's POVM; for mutually orthogonal members, probabilities add.

**The dilemma [D].** "Several distinct canonical endpoints constitute one observable event" therefore has four readings:

1. The endpoint differences are physical **and registered** — orthogonal as far as the detector and environment are concerned. Then they are which-path information, and the alternatives add incoherently.
2. They are physical but **unregistered at the resolution in play**: the detector's POVM, or the environment's record, does not separate them, so $\langle\Psi_2|E_y|\Psi_1\rangle\neq0$ and the cross term survives. This is exactly what resolution-limited agreeing structure points to, and it needs no change to quantum mechanics.
3. They are **gauge**, a redundancy of description. Then there is no physical spread to exploit.
4. The composition rule of quantum mechanics changes. That is genuinely new physics, facing every precision interference test there is.

**Two ways through [D].** The cleanest is the holonomy of a *phase*. When two alternatives reach the same final state along different base paths, their relative phase is the holonomy around the loop they enclose — the Aharonov–Bohm pattern, physical even though the connection itself is not. **The Hopf-phase direction can carry the proposal this way**: same observable endpoint, different canonical phase, observable as an interference shift. That requires light to couple to the Hopf connection, which version 16 forbids — its Maxwell field is $U(1)_H$-invariant — so it is a genuine version-17 modification of the optical sector, not a consequence of the architecture (§XIII.8). The second is reading 2 above: canonical endpoints that differ physically but are not separated by the detector or environment at the resolution in play. The meridian direction can contribute this way while its displacement lies below what anything registers, and ceases to once the displacement is recorded, when its signature becomes decoherence rather than new images. The Aethic determinacy criterion $\mathrm{Det}_R(\xi_1,\xi_2)$ is then best defined through the normalized POVM overlap $\langle\Psi_2|E_y|\Psi_1\rangle$, with rules A and B as its coarse readout. *An earlier statement that only phase-type holonomy survives inside standard quantum mechanics is withdrawn* **[R]**.

**Signatures [S].** Phase-type holonomy would appear as path-dependent interference phases in closed optical loops, beyond the known Sagnac and gravitational phases — tightly constrained locally. Position-type spread would appear as a loss of fringe visibility accumulating with path length — constrained by long-baseline interferometry. Neither is multiple imaging: ordinary lensing gives one source many images; this gives one image many canonical histories.

## XIII.4 Agreeing structure [T/D]

**Rendering as a correspondence [T].** A map cannot send one point to two. If one underlying point can appear twice in a rendered space, the primitive object is a correspondence — an incidence space of appearances with two legs,

$$\mathfrak X\xleftarrow{\;s\;}\mathcal I_U\xrightarrow{\;t\;}V_U$$

with $s(i_1) = s(i_2) = x$ while $t(i_1)\neq t(i_2)$. Fundamental identity and rendered location come apart. The version-16 seed is the case in which the underlying space is the gravity slice and each class appears as a point and its twin (§XIII.1). The substrate is written $\mathfrak X$ so as not to change what $E$ means.

**What each appearance keeps [D].** Give appearance $i$ a kernel $K_i\subset T_x\mathfrak X$ of forgotten directions, so that its rendered tangent space is $T_x\mathfrak X/K_i$. Three levels of information then sit in a lattice, $K_1\cap K_2\subset K_1,\,K_2\subset K_1+K_2$:

| quotient | what it holds |
|:--|:--|
| $T_x\mathfrak X/(K_1\cap K_2)$ | what the two appearances reveal together |
| $T_x\mathfrak X/K_i$ | what appearance $i$ shows by itself |
| $\mathcal A(i_1,i_2) = T_x\mathfrak X/(K_1+K_2)$ | **the agreeing structure** — what both retain, and so the only ground on which they can be compared |

with $\dim\mathcal A = \dim\mathfrak X-\dim K_1-\dim K_2+\dim(K_1\cap K_2)$. In terms of observables, if $\mathcal O_i$ are those surviving appearance $i$, agreement lives in $\mathcal O_1\cap\mathcal O_2$. **Which-path distinguishability between two appearances is governed by how differently they quotient, not by any separation of underlying points.** And the question "would humans have realized it by now?" has a precise answer: an inhabitant who sees only $T/K_1$ in one place and $T/K_2$ in another has no internal reason to identify them — exactly as with the logo.

## XIII.5 Centrades: fields as unfinished reductions [T/S/O]

**A centrade [D].** Provisionally, a centrade is a local carrier of several potentially incompatible reductions. Centrade $C_i$ has a space $P_i$ of unpacking states; a global unpacking $U = (u_i)$ must satisfy compatibility conditions $\mathcal C_\alpha(U) = 0$, and the admissible unpackings

$$\mathscr U_{\rm adm} = \Big\{U\in\prod_iP_i\;:\;\mathcal C_\alpha(U) = 0\Big\}$$

do not form a product. **That non-product structure is the budget**; no additive conservation law is assumed. The Hopf-sign condition of §V.4 is a verified miniature (§XIII.1). Equivalently, the admissible unpackings form a constraint-satisfaction problem: put a hyperedge around every set of resolutions that cannot coexist, and a rendering is a selection containing no complete hyperedge. *Packing* then means leaving a distinction unresolved in the invariant description; *unpacking* means selecting enough relational information for it to appear in a rendering; and $Q$ and $a$ become coordinates on a rendering rather than labels upstairs. A continuous version — unpacking degrees $u_i\in[0,1]$ under a joint constraint such as $\sum_{ij}W_{ij}u_iu_j\le K$ — is equally admissible; the form of the constraint is open.

**Fields as residual obstructions [T/S].** The reversal — a field measures what failed to unpack, not something packed in — has standard precedents. An almost complex structure fails to be integrable by exactly its Nijenhuis tensor; a $G$-structure fails to be torsion-free by its intrinsic torsion; a connection fails to be flat by its curvature. In each case the "field" is the obstruction to completing a reduction **[T]**. The proposal is that charge and spin are obstructions of this kind — $Q\sim{\rm Obs}_Q(U)$ and $a\sim{\rm Obs}_K(U)$, each vanishing when its channel is completely unpacked **[S]**.

**The Kerr–Newman budget [T/S].** Kerr–Newman's horizon condition, $a^2+Q^2+\delta^2 = M^2$ (§X.10), puts the family on a unit sphere in $(a,Q,\delta)/M$ — the charged unit circle of §X.0 extended by a rotational axis. Schwarzschild sits at $\delta = M$, fully unpacked in both channels; extremal Reissner–Nordström at $Q = M$; extremal Kerr at $a = M$. Reading this as a competition among reductions is new physics; general relativity supplies only the identity.

**A budget that black-hole physics already has [T/V].** Two established structures treat spin and charge on one footing. The horizon function factorizes as $\Delta = (r-r_+)(r-r_-)$ with $r_+r_- = a^2+Q^2$ and $(r_+-r_-)/2 = \delta$, for Kerr, Reissner–Nordström and Kerr–Newman alike: the product of the horizon radii, $a^2+Q^2$, is a horizon-product invariant — not to be confused with irreducible mass, which is fixed by the area. And the Christodoulou–Ruffini formula,

$$M^2 = \Big(M_{\rm irr}+\frac{Q^2}{4M_{\rm irr} }\Big)^2+\frac{J^2}{4M_{\rm irr}^2}$$

splits a hole's mass into an irreducible part, fixed by the horizon area that no classical process can decrease, and extractable charge and spin energies. Reversible processes strip the charge and spin down to a Schwarzschild hole of mass $M_{\rm irr}$ (checked numerically across the family). That is an existing, physical "unpacking budget" with an invariant.

> **Which invariant does unpacking preserve? [V/D].** The chiral weight rapidity holds $\delta$ fixed; physical processes hold the horizon area fixed; and the two disagree. Boosting a hole with $Q/M = 0.8$ to $\mu = 0$ at fixed $\delta$ shrinks its horizon area from $32.2$ to $18.1$ (with $G = c = 1$ and initial mass $1$), ending at a Schwarzschild mass of $0.60$; reversible extraction keeps the area and ends at $0.80$. So an unpacking at fixed $\delta$ cannot be a physical process within one rendering — the area theorem forbids it — and can only be a change of rendering. An unpacking at fixed area is a physical process, and ties the budget to the second law of black-hole mechanics. The theory must say which one its unpacking is.

**The chiral weight rapidity [D/O].** The quantity §X.0 needs is not a modulus of spinor representatives: any ratio $\lambda_+/\lambda_-$ of representative scalings is exactly the projective redundancy of the chiral bilinear, $(o_-,\tilde o_+)\mapsto(\lambda o_-,\lambda^{-1}\tilde o_+)$. It is a **chiral weight rapidity**. First construct $m_\pm$ as gauge-invariant scalar data of the two chiral solutions — residues, charges, asymptotic invariants, or whatever the construction produces; then study the boost $\mathcal B_\beta:(m_+,m_-)\mapsto(e^\beta m_+,e^{-\beta}m_-)$, under which $\mu = 0$ is balanced and chargeless and $|\mu|$ measures the imbalance. Only then is it meaningful to ask whether the modulus of some canonical $\mathbb{C}^\times$ geometrically parameterizes $\beta$.

**Chiral solution weight is not spinor normalization [V/D].** The reciprocal rescaling of the two weights is exactly the projective freedom of a chiral spinor pair, and it fixes only their product; were it gauge, the mass would be unphysical. So wherever the construction reproduces Reissner–Nordström, $m_\pm$ must be separately invariant constituent data and the rapidity a physical deformation, with only the swap $m_+\leftrightarrow m_-$ — that is, $Q\to-Q$ — preserving the gravitational data (§X.0). Kim's construction supports chiral constituents and charged chiral dyons, but it does not by itself identify $m_\pm$ with invariant constituent weights. That identification is the theorem target.

## XIII.6 Many rendered spacetimes [S/O]

**The renderer [D].** A renderer $\mathcal R:\mathscr U_{\rm adm}\to\mathfrak M_{\rm vis}$ assigns each admissible unpacking $U$ a rendered spacetime $V_U$. Ours is one point of $\mathfrak M_{\rm vis}$, not the substrate. The representational base of version 16 becomes one member of a family, $\{B_{\rm rep}^{(\sigma)}\}$, and the visual world is $B_{\rm vis} = B_{\rm rep}^{(\sigma_*)}$ for a rendering selected by multiplicity rather than by a new local law.

**Why ours [S].** Many microscopic unpackings render the same macroscopic world. Its multiplicity, $\Omega[V] = \mu\big(\mathcal R^{-1}([V])\big)$, and entropy $S[V] = \log\Omega[V]$ could make one rendering overwhelmingly represented. The tower picture adds a second factor, **relational complexity**. A single-file tower has many nodes but no cycles — its first Betti number $\beta_1 = E-V+C$ is zero — while a hexagonal sheet has an extensive number of independent cycles, and with them the redundancy that lets a pattern maintain itself. Visibility would track the capacity for redundantly supported relational structure, not the amount of underlying stuff. The author's sharper form is *rooted*: the output visual spacetime is the highest-complexity subgraph running through an axiomatically fixed centrade — the observer's own — schematically $\mathcal C_R(U;c) = \log N_R(U;c)$, counting consistent relational configurations within graph radius $R$ of the root $c$. Losing a direction is then more like discarding dimensionality than scrambling: the towers are present but relationally too poor to register. And processes involving few centrades — as orbital mechanics and single quantum particles escape Boltzmann's arrow — would be comparatively immune to this selection, which is the intuition behind the diagram analogy below **[S]**. An observer-persistence functional $\mathscr P(O,U)$ — how well the relations constituting a complex self-maintaining pattern survive unpacking $U$ — can stand in for talk of consciousness: in other renderings the underlying material persists, but no coherent pattern of "you" does.

**What stays invariant [O].** Is $B_{\rm grav}[U] = B_{\rm grav}$ for every $U$, as in the miniature of §XIII.1; only isomorphic; or itself rendered? Is $E$ invariant, or a rendered descendant of a deeper $\mathfrak X$? These are the architectural questions of the branch.

**The diagram analogy [S].** A Feynman diagram is a term in the expansion of an amplitude, not a trajectory. The disciplined form of the analogy is that rendered classical spacetime is to the substrate as the semiclassical, tree-level description is to the full sum, with quantum corrections as residual sensitivity to structure that the macroscopic rendering suppresses. Both the Dirac electron and a Kerr–Newman hole have $g = 2$ classically, and the electron's anomaly $a_e$ comes from loops. That coincidence is a signpost, not evidence; the conjecture would be earned only by deriving $a_e$ from unpacking sectors.

## XIII.7 The galactic plane, and the orbital flip [T/V/D]

**The plane as a fixed set [T].** On $S^3\subset\mathbb{R}^4$ the involution $\jmath(x_1,x_2,x_3,x_4) = (x_1,x_2,x_3,-x_4)$ squares to one and fixes a great two-sphere, locally a plane. An isotropic source upstairs with a planar visible locus is therefore kinematically possible; why luminous matter would select the fixed set is open.

**The orbital flip, computed [V].** The intuition — at the two turning points per radial period, where velocity is perpendicular to position, a star is rendered on the other side — has two readings that differ in the plane. "The opposite radial side", $\mathbf x\mapsto-\mathbf x$, is a half-turn and preserves handedness; a genuine "handedness flip" is a reflection. Both were integrated on precessing orbits in a flat-rotation-curve potential, and on a disk of 3000 phase-mixed orbits:

| | (i) opposite radial side, a half-turn | (ii) handedness flip, reflection across the radial line |
|:--|:--|:--|
| a single orbit | keeps circulating prograde, but jumps $2r$ across the centre at every turning point — continuous only in a two-sheeted glued space | **collapses exactly onto one fixed half-arc**: a non-precessing spiral segment, traced back and forth |
| a disk of stars | cannot create structure: the rendering commutes with rotations, so an axisymmetric disk stays axisymmetric; it acts only on odd-$m$ patterns | no global pattern either; and every star reverses its rendered azimuthal motion each half radial period |

Reading (ii) does something remarkable to single orbits. In any spherical potential, consecutive half-arcs of an orbit are mirror images across their shared apsidal line, so a rosette is literally built from reflections — and the handedness flip applies exactly those reflections in reverse. Each star's rendered path becomes one stationary spiral segment, spanning about half a turn in this theory's closed-arena potential **[V]**. But it pays with motion: for half of every radial period a star would appear to move backward, so the rendered disk would show no net rotation — contradicted by Doppler rotation curves and by stellar proper motions, which show coherent rotation. Reading (i) keeps the motion but, commuting with rotations, cannot turn an axisymmetric disk into a spiral **[D]**, as the simulation confirms: all low-order Fourier amplitudes stay at the noise level.

**The author's construction: a third reading [V].** The intended operation is neither of these. Cut the rosette at a pericentre and the following apocentre, and flip one half about the axis through the centre *perpendicular* to the apsidal radius, sending each turning point to its antipode. Because the two halves of any orbit are mirror images across their apsidal line, this turns one radial period into **a point-symmetric pair of identical arcs — two spirals** (exact to $10^{-12}$). Outgoing halves are leading-shaped and ingoing halves trailing-shaped, so trailing arms, as observed, require keeping the ingoing halves and flipping the outgoing ones. Seen from a fixed side the flipped stars orbit backward; done as a turn-over through the normal, the flipped half is simply seen from behind, which needs the twist to live in the readout rather than in visual space (§XIII.8). The jump across the centre is meant to be no motion at all but a projective gluing of the visual space — two appearances of one canonical path, as in §XIII.4. Like the other readings it commutes with rotations, so it turns *correlated* orbit orientations into two-armed patterns but cannot create them from a symmetric disk.

So **no reading of the flip makes spirals from a symmetric disk**. What survives is the observation that a handedness flip freezes rosettes into spiral segments — which a subtler rendering, treating positions and velocities differently or not commuting with rotations, would have to reconcile with coherent rotation **[O]**.

## XIII.8 Readout monodromy, closure defects, and a dipole clue — and whether they are one structure [T/V/D/S/O]

*This section formalizes the latest intuitions, then tests the claim that matters most: that they form one structure rather than a collection of analogies.*

**The readout package [D].** Let $\mathscr E$ be invariant canonical structure, $U$ a canonical flow, $\mathscr S$ a space of unpacking macrostates, and $\Pi_s:\mathscr E\to V_s$ the rendering for macrostate $s$ — branchwise, in the sense of §XIII.2 — with differential $P_x = d\Pi_s|_x$. A visible velocity is then $v_{\rm vis} = P_xU_x$, and the directions hidden from view are $\mathcal K_x = \ker P_x$. Three intuitions take a clean form.

- *The veering normal.* A lift $\widetilde n_x$ of the visible normal cannot itself be hidden, since $P_x\widetilde n_x = n_{\rm vis}\neq0$. The consistent form uses a preferred canonical line field $L_x = {\rm span}(m_x)$: near the disk its readout is the normal, $P_xm_x\parallel n_{\rm vis}$; farther out the alignment fails, or $\|P_xm_x\|\to0$ and the line becomes hidden. Nothing rotates into the kernel; a local identification between a canonical line and the rendered normal fails to extend. The author's question — whether the reverse half of an orbit *is* the hidden normal — has a precise form here: one smooth canonical continuation can be rendered as an orbital branch near the disk and as a hidden direction elsewhere; near us the two labels coincide, and farther out they can separate **[S/O]**. *(Corrected in 17.1* **[R]***.)*
- *The apsidal flip as monodromy.* Across the turning surfaces $p_r = 0$ the readout continues as $\Pi_{s,+} = \Pi_{s,-}\circ\tau$. The canonical orbit stays smooth, and its rendering passes from one representative to the other, giving the two arcs of §XIII.7; as a turn-over, the flip keeps each star's rotation consistent with its own local normal. The twist must live in the readout, not in visual space: an orientable plane in an orientable three-space always has an untwisted normal **[T]**.
- *The budget as an obstruction.* Local unpackings $s_i$ with transition functions $g_{ij}$ fail to assemble globally when the cocycle $g_{ij}g_{jk}g_{ki}$ is nontrivial **[T]** — the mature form of "whack-a-mole". The Hopf-sign condition of §V.4 is a verified instance.

**Closure defects [T/D].** For two base paths contributing to one rendered event, the relative closure defect is $\mathcal C(\gamma_1,\gamma_2) = H(\gamma_1)^{-1}H(\gamma_2)$, with $H$ the transport in the readout's structure group — a sign, a Hopf phase, or $\mathbb{C}^\times$. For two coherent legs weighted $e^{\pm\mu}$ with relative phase $\chi$, the combined brightness is

$$I\;\propto\;2\cosh2\mu+2\cos\chi$$

which is dark at $\chi = \pi$ only when the legs are balanced, $\mu = 0$. An involution fixes its fixed set pointwise, but its differential need not act trivially there: the plane reflection fixes every point of the galactic plane while acting as $-1$ on its normal. So whether a sign defect built from it vanishes on the plane depends on the representation that carries the defect **[T]**.

> **What a closure defect can and cannot do to a single ray [D].** A pure scalar phase or sign cannot attenuate the total intensity of a single isolated ray: a ray paired with itself always closes. And generic nontrivial winding sectors differ in optical length by order $\ell$ — up to $2\pi\ell/c\approx3\times10^5$ years of delay — vastly exceeding the coherence of astronomical light, nanoseconds or at most seconds for masers; exceptional degenerate or near-degenerate paths, the antipodal configuration being the obvious case, must be treated separately. So large-scale darkness cannot generically rest on coherent cancellation, and the two-leg formula describes laboratory interference rather than a dark sky. Three routes to darkness remain, none yet derived: **non-unitary leakage**, a $\mathbb{C}^\times$ modulus dimming a ray by $e^{-2\mu}$; **unitary mode conversion**, rotating amplitude into polarizations, internal states or modes excluded from the rendered channel — nothing destroyed, the renderer simply ceasing to expose it, which fits the centrade picture; and **closure obstruction**, below. An earlier suggestion that the two-leg formula could produce the dark normal, and a later one that only a non-unitary modulus remains, are both corrected here **[R]**.

**Closure obstruction: the natural primitive [D/S/O].** Three failure modes should be kept apart:

| mode | what fails |
|:--|:--|
| **interference** | two admissible histories reach one configuration and cancel, $A_1+A_2\approx0$ |
| **quotient blindness** | $q(x) = q(y)$ although $x\neq y$ — the original seed |
| **closure obstruction** | each leg is admissible, but no rendered endpoint satisfies both legs' identifications at once |

With an admissibility relation $\mathcal A(h_+,h_-;x)$ saying whether two legs close to the rendered event $x$, visibility needs $\{x:\mathcal A(h_+,h_-;x) = 1\}\neq\varnothing$, and **nonclosure is that set being empty**. Nothing cancels; the pair simply has no common rendering. This is the natural form of the intuition that light "cannot close" along the normal: near the plane the closure set is nonempty, beyond some change in agreeing structure it empties, and the plane could be special as the fixed locus of whatever involution controls the paired readout — an implementation target, not a derived fact. In the author's picture the privileged rendering itself — the high-multiplicity macrostate of §XIII.6 — would fix which direction is privileged for closure, tying the optical asymmetry to the selection of the rendering rather than to a separate law **[S]**. Interference may appear downstream in an amplitude description, but it is not the primitive. **This is a definition, not yet a mechanism**: the compatibility structure that empties the set, and why it does so along the normal and not in the plane, remain to be constructed **[O]**. It must also leave the observed sky toward the galactic poles bright, including the resolved stars of Sculptor at 86 kpc and Leo I at 254 kpc, and the resolved galaxies beyond.

**Pairs, and a blur [T/S].** The author's pair intuition — visual space always built from *pairs* of underlying points, as the Born rule builds probabilities from an amplitude and its conjugate — has a standard form. A quantum state's density matrix is a function on pairs of points; the Born rule reads its diagonal; the Wigner function is built from the pairs' midpoints and separations; and smoothing it with a Gaussian gives the Husimi function, which is what a finite-resolution instrument records **[T]**. In agreeing-structure terms, the width of that window is set by the kernel of forgotten directions. That suggests a second reading of the boundary at the circumgalactic medium: not emptiness but *merging*, where the forgotten kernel grows until distinct canonical points can no longer be told apart — a blur of the output rather than a gap in it **[S]**. The same constraint applies: the sky toward the galactic poles is sharp, so any blur must act on canonical content that never reaches the visual space as images.

**The Hopf firewall [D].** Version 16's Maxwell field is invariant under $U(1)_H$. Any use of Hopf holonomy to control light — here or in §XIII.3 — is a version-17 modification of the optical sector, not a consequence of the architecture.

**A dipole clue [V/S].** The Solar System's velocity relative to the cosmic microwave background — $369.82$ km/s toward Galactic longitude $264.0^\circ$, latitude $48.3^\circ$ (Planck) — has an in-plane part of $246.2$ km/s lying **$3.4^\circ$ from exactly reversed solar orbital motion**. That motion is $248.5$ km/s along the rotation, from the proper motion of Sgr A*, plus small radial and vertical components; the vector mismatch is $15$ km/s. In standard terms, the Galaxy moves about $565$ km/s toward longitude $266^\circ$, latitude $28$–$29^\circ$, relative to the background, and its in-plane part is **$1.99$ times the Sun's orbital speed, pointing against the rotation**. Rescaling so the in-plane parts match and then removing the reversed solar motion leaves $286$ km/s pointing $3.0^\circ$ from the north Galactic pole. That residual is not a third coincidence: once the in-plane parts are matched, its near-normality follows from the alignment already noted. The independent curiosities are the $3.4^\circ$ directional match and the near-equal magnitudes, $246.2$ against $248.5$ km/s. Its statistical significance is open **[O]**. A meaningful number needs a stated null ensemble — the distribution of Galactic peculiar velocities, conditioning on the observed speed, a tolerance metric, and a penalty for the other alignments that might have caught the eye — and an angular coincidence alone is far weaker than any figure that also uses the magnitude. It is a clue, not evidence, and coincidence remains the default.

In the readout language the clue reads cleanly. The readout $P = T\circ\tfrac12(1+j)$ — keep the in-plane part, reverse it, hide the normal — carries the in-plane background velocity onto the Sun's orbital velocity within $15$ km/s. If every star shared one upstairs velocity and the readout turned with each star's position about the centre, every star would show the same tangential speed: **a flat rotation curve at $246$ km/s**. Two cautions follow. The normal part is not invisible, since it is exactly what the background measures; and a velocity shared by every star is already invisible to relative stellar kinematics in standard physics. So the genuinely new content is the reversal of the in-plane part. A rotation curve that rises in the middle, epicycles and velocity dispersion would all require $P_x$ to carry structure, which must be derived rather than fitted. The background itself stays outside the one-arena model, in the spirit of Part XII. **A rule for this clue [D]:** any preferred axis must be derived first and only then compared with the dipole and the low multipoles; the observed direction must never be used to construct it.

**Is it one structure? Four tests [V/D].**

| test | result |
|:--|:--|
| **one involution** | **no literal one.** The twin acts freely on the arena; the plane involution fixes a great two-sphere; the flip, as a turn-over, fixes a great circle; and the dipole reversal is not a map of positions at all, since it reverses velocity in place. Fixed sets are invariant under any change of frame, so no single involution acting on one manifold in one representation can play these roles. One upstairs symmetry inducing different effective maps after different reductions is not excluded |
| **one small group** | **partly.** On the states where each rule acts, the flip equals the disk half-turn $k$ followed by time reversal $T$, and the dipole readout is $T\circ\tfrac12(1+j)$. So $T$ links the flip and the dipole, and the plane reflection $j$ links the dipole's hidden normal, the bright plane and the rotation-consistent flip. But $k$, $T$ and $j$ are simply the discrete operations of a rotating disk, each phenomenon picks its own combination, and the twin needs a fourth generator |
| **one defect seen twice** | **no, for the natural candidates.** The rosette's own sign rule — one flip per radial turning point — gives paired light paths a relative sign fixed by global geometry: never dark for the two ways round a great circle, always dark for a path and its twin's. The plane-crossing rule darkens every off-plane source, the nearest stars included. Neither singles out the distant normal |
| **darkness at all** | **not by a pure phase or sign.** Three routes remain — non-unitary leakage, unitary mode conversion, closure obstruction — and none is yet derived |

> **Verdict [D].** Not yet one structure. The proposal is a common language with **two genuine shared pieces** — time reversal, shared by the flip and the dipole readout, and the plane reflection, shared by the hidden normal, the bright plane and the rotation-consistent flip — and **three candidate routes** to darkness, none yet derived: non-unitary leakage, unitary mode conversion and closure obstruction. No single literal involution realizes twin, plane and flip, though one upstairs symmetry inducing them differently after different reductions is not excluded; and the charge modulus of §X.0 shares a group with the optical modulus, not an element: nothing yet maps a hole's $\mu$ onto the readout's. A genuine unification would be one object from which each phenomenon's combination *follows* rather than being chosen — the test the next abstraction has to pass.

## XIII.9 Two readouts, a gravitational firewall, and prospect holonomy [T/V/D/S/O]

*Two further refinements: gravity as a readout of centrades rather than of everything that carries energy, and the normal-direction effect moved out of optics and into the lift itself.*

**Centrades carry localization; propagators carry relations [S].** Between an emission centrade and an absorption centrade, the propagation relating them would not itself be a gravitational centre. Stated carefully, this does not say propagation lacks gravity — its stress still curves spacetime — but that **it carries no independent gravitational datum**: no autonomous Weyl curvature of its own, only what its stress forces and what centrades and boundary data supply.

**In the author's framing [S].** The Coulomb field of a charged hole, its centre of mass, and the determinate nodes that exchange an electron would all be centrades. A photon of braking radiation adds information, by adding centrades near its source. And naive semiclassical gravity — sourcing the metric with the expectation value of an electron pure state's stress between fixed endpoints — would not apply: the middle of the process would obey the zero-type constraint below, while its ends, as centrades, gravitate as individually isotropic sources. A field that is "informationally monolithic", one irreducible centrade, then reproduces standard general relativity. The author's conclusion is the crossed table below: gravity can operate without visual matter content, and visual content can exist without an independent gravitational centre.

**What standard relativity allows [T/V].** Maxwell stress is trace-free, so with no cosmological constant $R = 0$ and $R_{ab} = 8\pi G\,T^{\rm EM}_{ab}$, and the Weyl tensor carries the rest of the curvature. Computed on exact plane waves:

| wave | Ricci curvature | Weyl curvature |
|:--|:--|:--|
| electromagnetic, isotropic profile | pure radiation, $R_{uu}\neq0$ | **zero** — conformally flat |
| vacuum gravitational | zero | nonzero |
| electromagnetic, anisotropic profile | $R_{uu}\neq0$ | nonzero, proportional to the anisotropy |

So radiation *can* curve spacetime with no Weyl curvature at all, but not in general. The contracted Bianchi identity also makes gradients of stress a source of Weyl curvature, so $C_{abcd} = 0$ cannot be imposed on generic radiation. The candidate rule is therefore weaker: **no autonomous Weyl datum on centrade-free sectors**, with Ricci curvature fully allowed **[S]**.

**The obstacle [O].** In general relativity the split of Weyl curvature into a sourced part and a free part is not local: it depends on boundary conditions, such as the absence of incoming radiation. A gravitational wave far from its source is locally free Weyl curvature, yet retardedly sourced by the binary that emitted it. So the rule says something new only if the canonical structure supplies a *local*, covariant split; otherwise it restates retarded boundary conditions.

**Reissner–Nordström as two readouts of one centrade [S].** A charged hole is not merely field lying around: it has an irreducible centre. The proposal reads its metric and its Coulomb field as two readouts of one centrade, $\mathfrak c\mapsto\big(g_{\rm RN},\,F_{\rm Coulomb}\big)$, rather than as the chain $F\to T[F]\to g$. That gives intuition (B) of §X.0 a home, and inherits its double-counting question. A centrade is then best defined provisionally as **a minimal canonical agreement object, indecomposable under the permitted readouts**, with charges, fields and points as its representations **[D/S]**.

**Two readouts, and the crossed cases [D/S].** If the invariant structure is read out twice — once as a visual, localizing spacetime and once as a gravitational one — the two need not lose the same information:

| | visual localization | independent gravitational centre |
|:--|:--|:--|
| ordinary matter | yes | yes |
| the barren twin (§V.3) | no | yes — already in version 16 |
| propagation between centrades | yes, in amplitudes | no, though its stress still gravitates |

The preferred ordering makes gravity a quotient *of each rendering*, $\mathfrak X\xrightarrow{U_u}V_u\xrightarrow{r_u}B_{ {\rm grav},u}$ — the first arrow branchwise, globally a correspondence — compatible with the invariant quotient $q$ in the sense $r_u\circ U_u = \bar U_u\circ q$. The strongest case, $r_u\circ U_u = q$ for every rendering — every visual spacetime a resolution of one invariant gravity base — is already realized by the $2^N$ occupancy renderings of §XIII.1 **[V]**. A refinement multiplicity $\mathcal K(\mathfrak c,U)$, counting the canonical refinements compatible with a readout, with $S_{\rm unpack} = \log\mathcal K$, is the object any future measure principle would act on; no probability rule is assumed **[D/O]**.

**Prospect holonomy: make a lift stop closing, not the normal disappear [T/V/D/O].** Gravity along the normal is measured: the Galaxy's vertical force is determined directly, and gravitating mass away from the plane is inferred, though the shape of its dark component remains model-dependent. The author's ordering argument is kept: the reduction that treats the normal specially happens *before* gravity's quotient, so gravity — a quotient of each visual spacetime — must inherit a correspondent of it, and the effect cannot be purely optical, added after gravity is fixed. Its premise needs care. The author reads the Galaxy's well as a disk's, with no extra content as if the missing normal-direction matter formed an isotropic ball; the standard reading, just noted, infers a dark component well away from the plane — and in this model the closure plateau itself acts isotropically. So what gravity inherits cannot be the disappearance of gravity along the normal. It can be how visual representatives glue over gravitational classes: a lift that stops closing, not a normal that disappears. Let the visual representatives of each gravitational class form a locally trivial bundle; transport around a loop that closes downstairs acts on them by a holonomy $H(\gamma)$, and **non-closure is $H(\gamma)\neq1$**. Three results fix what this can and cannot do:

1. **Version 16 already has one [V].** The twin double cover: a non-contractible loop of the gravity slice lifts to a path from a point to its twin. It is trivial for every loop smaller than the arena and nontrivial only at arena scale — the "closed near, open far" pattern — but it is not direction-selective.
2. **No flat bundle can be selective [T].** The gravity slice carries two flat $\mathbb Z_2$ bundles, and restricting to the plane's projective two-plane loses nothing, because every non-contractible loop can be drawn inside the plane. A flat holonomy depends only on a loop's class, so it cannot close in the plane and fail along the normal; the same holds for flat $U(1)$ bundles. Direction selectivity needs **curvature**, or a locus removed from the arena off the plane.
3. **Curvature odd under the plane reflection is selective [D/V].** Such curvature restricts to zero on the plane: with $i$ the inclusion of the fixed plane, $i^*\Omega = i^*j^*\Omega = -i^*\Omega$, so $i^*\Omega = 0$, while away from the plane tangential components can survive as odd functions of the normal coordinate. So the connection restricted to the plane is *flat*, while loops that leave the plane enclose flux. Flat is not yet trivial: on a plane that is not simply connected, a flat connection can keep monodromy around non-contractible loops, so exact closure of every planar loop additionally requires trivial restricted holonomy — automatic on the two-sphere upstairs, not on the projective plane downstairs **[T]**. And descended to the gravity slice, the plane reflection fixes the plane's projective two-plane *and* one isolated point on the normal axis, a quarter-circumference from the centre — worth recording if the reflection becomes fundamental here **[V]**. In an explicit local toy, loops in the plane have holonomy exactly zero, a horizontal loop at height $z_0$ picks up flux proportional to $z_0$, and a vertical loop reaching height $h$ picks up flux proportional to $h$. If the connection is weak, non-closure sets in only where the enclosed flux reaches order one: closed near us, open far out along the normal.

> **Status [D/O].** Result 3 is the geometric *shape* of a mechanism for "the light cannot close far along the normal" — more than the definition of §XIII.8, less than a derivation. Its free choices are named: the connection on the prospect bundle, its strength scale, and why its curvature is odd under the plane reflection — the last of which version 21.4 gives a canonical enforcer — the fiber-inverting lift that the Chern class forces — conditional on the connection's equivariance under it, §XVI.8. Deriving them is the task, and whatever is derived must leave the sky toward the galactic poles bright — resolved stars at 86 and 254 kpc, and resolved galaxies beyond. It is also the author's "light searches more once flatness is relaxed", made directional: by §XIII.2's criterion, new optical behaviour appears exactly where the holonomy of closable loops exceeds resolution — here selectively along the normal.

## XIII.10 Conjugate-path optics: when the isolated ray ceases to be the whole stationary object [T/V/D/S/O]

*"A photon follows a null geodesic" is a geometric-optics limit, not an ontology. This section, from the author's conjugate-path brief, replaces the isolated ray by the class it is the generic member of, and does the first four calculations the brief asks for.*

**The gate [T/D].** Let $\gamma$ be a stationary optical history between fixed readout endpoints, and $J$ an infinitesimal relative displacement between two candidate histories, with $J = 0$ at both ends. $J$ obeys the Jacobi equation of the *full* optical geometry, $D^2J/d\lambda^2+R(J,k)k = 0$, on the two-dimensional screen transverse to the null tangent. The **branching multiplicity** is

$$b(\gamma) = \dim\ker_D\mathcal J_\gamma$$

with fixed-endpoint boundary conditions. For $b = 0$ the ray is an isolated critical point of the optical action and ordinary geometric optics holds. For $b > 0$ the endpoint is conjugate along $\gamma$, the action's Hessian has a zero direction, and the stationary object is no longer a single ray. Curvature alone opens nothing: **conjugacy is the gate.** Its model-independent foundation is stated in §XV.0. The sharp detector is the Jacobi propagator $D$, with $D(0) = 0$ and $D'(0) = I$: $b > 0$ exactly when $\det D(\lambda_f) = 0$.

**Static form [T/V].** For the theory's static metric, $-N^2dt^2+\phi^4g_{\rm ref}$, rays are geodesics of the Fermat metric $\bar h = (\phi^4/N^2)\,g_{\rm ref}$, conformal to the reference metric. Near a secondary this is $(1+GM/2r)^6(1-GM/2r)^{-2}\,\delta$, whose weak-field refractive index is the standard $1+2GM/r$. The bare round metric is the control case only; once a secondary is present the Jacobi operator must be built from $\bar h$.

**Calculation 1 — the round arena [V].** On $S^3(\ell)$, $J''+J/\ell^2 = 0$ with $J(0) = J(L) = 0$ has a solution only for $L_n = n\pi\ell$, and both transverse directions are zero modes, so $b = 2$ at each. The first, $L_1 = \pi\ell$, is the antipode: **a source's first conjugate point is its twin.** There the critical set is not a marginal direction but a whole family — every great circle from $p$ reaches $-p$ — a Morse–Bott critical manifold of dimension two, equal to the nullity. On the gravity slice $\mathbb{RP}^3$, where $p$ and $-p$ are one point, every point's own conjugate locus at $L = \pi\ell$ is *itself*: the closed geodesics through $[p]$, one for each direction. The integer $n$ is a traversal index, not a quantum number.

**Calculation 3 — a weak secondary [V].** A point mass on the ray shifts the antipodal conjugate point by $\delta L = \mp(4GM/c^2)(\ell^2/b^2)\sin^2(s_L/\ell)$, earlier in the tangential direction and later in the radial: the point-lens shear splits the double zero mode into two simple ones. For the Galaxy's own mass at kiloparsec impact parameters the shift is parts per million of $\ell$, so the round case is a faithful guide to the arena's conjugate structure, while the split is generic. The invariant statement is the zero of the Jacobi determinant, not $L = n\pi\ell$.

**Calculation 4 — curved, but one path [V].** In the exact isotropic optical metric of the Sun, a grazing ray meets the axis $548$ AU behind it — the solar gravitational lens, $b^2/4GM$ — and a ray at three solar radii $4{,}900$ AU; at one astronomical unit the conjugate point lies $2.5\times10^7$ AU away. Radially neighbouring rays never cross at all. Within the solar system the tidal field bends every ray and $\det D\neq0$ for every endpoint pair: the gate stays shut.

**What is already standard [T].** None of this is new physics by itself. Path integrals sum histories; stationary phase selects rays; several rays may connect two endpoints; caustics and conjugate points are precisely where the isolated stationary-point approximation degenerates; the Van Vleck determinant and Maslov phases track it; and curved-spacetime Green functions carry the structure of every null geodesic and change character across caustics. "Allow more than one path at a conjugate point" is therefore not yet a claim.

**What Projective Gravity proposes [S].** The candidate content is a *canonical readout*, in the correspondence language of §XIII.2–XIII.4: the histories of one Jacobi-critical class are read as one photon channel because their readout data coincide, while distinct isolated rays remain distinct images. Stacked with the rest of Part XIII the division of labour is clean — **the Jacobi kernel decides when extra histories are available; the correspondence decides whether they map to one event; the detector's overlap decides whether they stay coherent; holonomy carries their relative data; the observable map says what a detector records.** Three levels are kept apart. As a *diagnostic* — $b$ and $\det D$ applied inside the theory — it is standard geometry. As the **Photon History Postulate** — *the semiclassical optical object for fixed readout endpoints is the maximal canonically compatible stationary history class, with one member away from conjugate structure* — it is a genuine proposal that changes the readout but not the composition rule. A *modified propagator* is reserved for the case in which the postulate predicts something ordinary Maxwell theory and quantum mechanics do not. And a zero mode is a degenerate stationary direction, not a stable equilibrium: whether a finite separated pair survives is decided by the cubic and quartic terms of the optical action, still to be computed, with no stabilizing term inserted by hand.

> **The first acceptance test [D].** Calculation 1 makes the optical cap problem (§XI.2, item 0) exact. On the gravity slice every source's light refocuses on the source itself after $\pi\ell/c\approx1.6\times10^5$ years, from every direction — a full-sky self-image of the Galaxy that no observation shows. So the antipodal, twin-located critical family is the one the readout must *not* render as an image. Whatever the Photon History Postulate says about that family is its first test; the closure obstruction and prospect holonomy of §XIII.8–XIII.9 are the tools available, and the twin legs of §XIII.8 are members of exactly this family.

> **The self-return test, quantified [V].** On the round arena the geodesic sphere of radius $s$ has area $4\pi\ell^2\sin^2(s/\ell)$, so a returning wavefront has exactly the area it had going out: the Sun's own light would come back after $2\pi\ell/c$ — $1.6\times10^5$ years at $\ell = 8$ kpc, $3.3\times10^5$ at $16$, while the first conjugate event, at the twin, comes after $\pi\ell/c$, half that — **at the full solar constant, from every direction** — about $10^8$ times the darkest night sky. Smearing the focus by the Galaxy's lumps displaces it by $4GM\ell^2/c^2b^2$, some $10^4$ AU for the Galaxy's bulk and $10^2$ AU for a passing star, which still leaves an isotropic solar-spectrum glow at one to $10^4$ times the night sky; dark-sky photometry attributes the night sky's continuum to zodiacal light, with its ecliptic dependence, and airglow, and shows no such glow. And every other star's return delivers at the Sun exactly its direct flux again, isotropically: a glow equal to the integrated starlight, some ten times the measured extragalactic background. **So light cannot propagate on the closed representational arena *as the visual space* and return.** This is the optical cap problem of §XI.2's item 0 in its sharpest form, and it leaves exactly two options: either the readout is noncompact in the sense that light does not come back — the escape pictures of §XIII.8–XIII.9 — or a return is *rendered elsewhere*, registered as light from an apparent distance $D_{\rm app}$ at whatever flux the observable map assigns — which — *if* $D_{\rm app}$ acts as a luminosity distance, a relation the branch has not yet derived — must be smaller than naive return by a factor of order $(D_{\rm app}/\ell)^2$, $10^7$ to $10^{11}$ for apparent distances of $10$ Mpc to $1$ Gpc, a conditional constraint on the map's joint output rather than a factor assigned by hand — with the observer's own return lying in the readout kernel. That is the requirement any return picture must meet.

**A modular-return branch, examined [S/O].** A proposal for a version 19 asks whether the antipodal family, broken by secondaries into discrete recurrent classes under a return map, could render *other galaxies* as recurrent photon histories of the one arena, with a unitary spectral-dilation readout $(U_\eta\psi)(\omega) = e^{\eta/2}\psi(e^\eta\omega)$ supplying $1+z = e^\eta$ and $(1+z)$ time dilation from one $\eta$. The self-return test does not settle it, because the proposal is a rendering, not a propagation; it fixes the price. On the ledger:

| for | against |
|:--|:--|
| the only concrete route yet to the other half of the wing's problem — where other galaxies come from in one arena | the returning photons converge canonically at the observer, yet must be registered at whatever flux the observable map assigns — smaller than naive return by a factor of order $(D_{\rm app}/\ell)^2$ for the sky to be dark, if $D_{\rm app}$ acts as a luminosity distance — a conditional joint constraint on the map's output, with no formalism yet |
| the Morse–Bott mechanism is real and computable: secondaries do break the round degeneracy **[V]** | each *recurrent class* must render with a galaxy's morphology, spectrum, population and independent variability; and the first-order count — about two stationary members per star, the rays through the stars and the saddles between — is a property of the sum's singular structure, not a galaxy count, which itself runs from $10^{11}$ to $10^{12}$; the return map's periodic orbits are the right object |
| the self-return test is a construction tool rather than a verdict: it asks for the invariant that puts the self-return class in the readout kernel **[D]** | in matching units the Galaxy's integrated starlight, $200$–$2{,}000$ nW m$^{-2}$ sr$^{-1}$ at optical wavelengths, is $20$ to $180$ times the measured optical background of $11.2\pm1.7$ nW m$^{-2}$ sr$^{-1}$, so the readout must send all but a few per cent of returned starlight into its kernel; and the images would be $\pi\ell/c$ to $2\pi\ell/c$ old — $0.8$ to $3.3\times10^5$ years across the candidate radii |
| one $\eta$ gives both the redshift and the $(1+z)$ time dilation, the test that tired light fails — a design success, not yet a physical one **[T]** | $\eta$ must be derived from the geometry, and the same map must yield flux, $D_L$, $D_A$, surface brightness and lensing jointly; and any increment that is a difference of endpoint potentials vanishes on a closed orbit, so a nonzero redshift needs a non-exact $\mathbb R_+$ connection, its per-return increment a Hubble constant inserted unless derived from $\ell$ and a recurrence number |

**Verdict:** the naive interpretation is killed; the rendering interpretation survives; opened as a ledger, not as a law, and developed as Part XIV. Its sharpest construction test is the observer's own return: a universal rule $\mathcal O(\Xi_{\rm self}) = \varnothing$ must follow from an invariant of the self-return class lying in the readout kernel, not from "do not render the Sun" put in by hand — and the closure-obstruction and correspondence machinery of §XIII.8–XIII.9 is where such an invariant would come from. Three layers are kept apart throughout: **canonical refocusing, visual image location, and source identity.**

**Strong lensing [D/O].** Ordinary lensing already supplies multiple rays, caustics and magnification, and the theory may use the same threshold — not a defect for a dark-sector model. It must then answer whether the rule merely reproduces lensing; what observable survives once lensing is subtracted; whether two lensed images stay two while two canonical histories stay one; and whether phase, visibility, magnification, delay, polarization or multiplicity changes.

**Invariants, and the open calculations [D/O].** For each reference history: $b(\gamma)$; the lowest fixed-endpoint eigenvalue $\lambda_0(\gamma)$, with the Lorentzian index treated properly rather than as an energy minimum; and the conjugacy detector $\det D(\lambda_f)$. Remaining: the second variation with gauge and reparametrization modes removed; the higher-order completion at a zero mode; the passage of a critical family through the correspondence and the detector's POVM; and at least one quantity — phase, visibility, magnification, arrival time, polarization or multiplicity — that differs from standard theory. Without the last, the branch is a powerful reinterpretation, not a law. The sentence it must eventually answer is: **what does the theory predict for a photon near a conjugate point that Maxwell theory plus quantum mechanics does not?**

## XIII.11 Status of the branch [D]
